========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, it's nice to meet you too, doctor, though to be perfectly honest, my husband is the one who really pushed for this appointment. But I suppose I’m here because... well, it all goes back to our house. Specifically, the kitchen. We decided to do a remodel, just a simple update, you know, but for nearly two years now—almost twenty-four whole months of this absolute nightmare—I’ve been absolutely sure that the contractor we hired is doing something completely underhanded. 

And it’s not just him, which is the really upsetting part. It’s some of the people right on my block, too. I’ve seen them out the window, talking by the mailboxes, looking over at my house and then suddenly stopping when I open the front door. They’ve been quietly working together to cheat us, I just know it. They are deliberately trying to keep my kitchen half-wrong on purpose. The cabinet doors don't line up right, the grout is completely the wrong shade, the trim isn't finished... they leave it like that on purpose, just stringing the job along to mock me. 

And my husband, bless his heart, he just doesn't want to see the reality of it. He keeps pulling out this thick folder of receipts, sitting me down at the dining table and pointing at the line items and the dates, trying to show me that everything is accounted for. But no matter how many receipts my husband shows me, or how many ridiculous excuses the contractor gives him over the phone—blaming supply chains or weather or whatever new lie he comes up with—it doesn't change what's actually happening. I know what I see, and I know they're all in on it together.

Energetic? Oh, heavens, no. No, not at all. If anything, I am just completely exhausted by this whole ordeal. I mean, I’m sixty-seven years old, I haven't worked in quite some time, and I certainly don't have the energy of a teenager anymore, that's for sure.

It's just because my mind won't shut off. I'm lying there worrying about what the contractor is going to mess up next, or I'm replaying what I saw out the window, wondering exactly what those neighbors were whispering about by the mailboxes. I just lie there in the dark, looking up at the ceiling, feeling my stomach tie itself in knots over that crooked cabinet hinge they refuse to fix. And my husband, he just sleeps right through it, snoring away, completely oblivious to it all. 

But I'm certainly not bouncing off the walls or getting up to scrub the floors at midnight or anything like that. I need my rest. By the time the 10 o'clock news is over, I am more than ready to turn in, maybe read a few pages of a book, and just close my eyes. So, no, I haven't felt unusually energetic in the slightest. This whole nightmare with the house has just completely drained whatever energy I do have, to be perfectly honest with you. It takes a lot out of you, having to be constantly on your guard in your own home.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Voices? Oh, no, no, nothing like that. I mean, I hear my husband snoring, like I said, and sometimes I hear the wind rattling that loose piece of siding the contractor promised to secure three months ago—which, of course, he hasn't done, and I'm sure he left it loose on purpose just so I'd have to listen to it every time there's a breeze. But actual voices? People talking when there's no one in the room? No, absolutely not. 

I know what I hear, just like I know what I see. When I hear those neighbors talking by the mailboxes, they are really there. I can see Mrs. Gable in her awful pink tracksuit, and Mr. Henderson with his little dog, and I can hear the murmur of their voices. I can't always make out the exact words, mind you, because they drop their voices on purpose when I open the door, but they are real people having a real conversation about me. 

But I certainly don't sit alone in my living room hearing phantom voices whispering in my ear. My hearing is perfectly fine, thank goodness. In fact, I had it tested just last year at Dr. Aris's office—my regular doctor, you know, over on Elm Street—and he said for a woman of sixty-seven, my hearing is quite sharp. Which is probably why I catch all the little sighs and muttered comments the contractor makes when he thinks I'm not paying attention. But no, doctor, I don't hear things that aren't there. I'm just very observant of the things that *are* there, even if my husband refuses to acknowledge them.

I can't always make out the exact words, mind you, because they drop their voices on purpose when I open the door, but they are real people having a real conversation about me. \n\nBut I certainly don't sit alone in my living room hearing phantom voices whispering in my ear. My hearing is perfectly fine, thank goodness. In fact, I had it tested just last year at Dr. Aris's office—my regular doctor, you know, over on Elm Street—and he said for a woman of sixty-seven, my hearing is quite sharp. Which is probably why I catch all the little sighs and muttered comments the contractor makes when he thinks I'm not paying attention. But no, doctor, I don't hear things that aren't there. I'm just very observant of the things that *are* there, even if my husband refuses to acknowledge them.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

I'm lying there worrying about what the contractor is going to mess up next, or I'm replaying what I saw out the window, wondering exactly what those neighbors were whispering about by the mailboxes. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh, well... no, I wouldn't say so. Not at all. I mean, my husband and I have our little disagreements, certainly. Just last week we had a bit of a row over how to organize the pantry—which, frankly, is a mess right now because the contractor left the shelving half-finished, just bare wood with nails sticking out, if you can believe it. But that's just normal married life, isn't it? Disagreeing about where the soup cans go. 

But as for big, strong beliefs that people say aren't true? No, nothing like that. I've always been a very practical, down-to-earth sort of person. I don't go in for conspiracy theories or wild ideas or anything of that nature. I just pay attention to what's going on around me. I pay my bills, I keep a tidy house—or at least I try to, despite the dust that contractor tracks in on purpose—and I mind my own business. 

It’s really just this one specific situation. It’s so glaringly obvious, you see. Anyone with eyes in their head would see exactly what I see if they just spent a week in my house, watching how these people operate. But aside from this whole mess with the kitchen and the neighbors whispering by the mailboxes... no, there's absolutely nothing else. I’m just a normal woman trying to get her kitchen finished, and being thwarted at every turn. That’s the long and short of it, really.

But actual voices? People talking when there's no one in the room? No, absolutely not. \n\nI know what I hear, just like I know what I see. When I hear those neighbors talking by the mailboxes, they are really there. I can see Mrs. Gable in her awful pink tracksuit, and Mr. Henderson with his little dog, and I can hear the murmur of their voices. I can't always make out the exact words, mind you, because they drop their voices on purpose when I open the door, but they are real people having a real conversation about me. \n\nBut I certainly don't sit alone in my living room hearing phantom voices whispering in my ear. My hearing is perfectly fine, thank goodness. In fact, I had it tested just last year at Dr. Aris's office—my regular doctor, you know, over on Elm Street—and he said for a woman of sixty-seven, my hearing is quite sharp. Which is probably why I catch all the little sighs and muttered comments the contractor makes when he thinks I'm not paying attention. But no, doctor, I don't hear things that aren't there. I'm just very observant of the things that *are* there, even if my husband refuses to acknowledge them.\n\nDoctor: Aside from this situation with the contractor and your neighbors, have you had any other strong beliefs that other people said were not true?\n\nPatient: Oh, well... no, I wouldn't say so. Not at all. I mean, my husband and I have our little disagreements, certainly. Just last week we had a bit of a row over how to organize the pantry—which, frankly, is a mess right now because the contractor left the shelving half-finished, just bare wood with nails sticking out, if you can believe it. But that's just normal married life, isn't it? Disagreeing about where the soup cans go. \n\nBut as for big, strong beliefs that people say aren't true? No, nothing like that. I've always been a very practical, down-to-earth sort of person. I don't go in for conspiracy theories or wild ideas or anything of that nature. I just pay attention to what's going on around me. I pay my bills, I keep a tidy house—or at least I try to, despite the dust that contractor tracks in on purpose—and I mind my own business. \n\nIt’s really just this one specific situation. It’s so glaringly obvious, you see. Anyone with eyes in their head would see exactly what I see if they just spent a week in my house, watching how these people operate. But aside from this whole mess with the kitchen and the neighbors whispering by the mailboxes... no, there's absolutely nothing else. I’m just a normal woman trying to get her kitchen finished, and being thwarted at every turn. That’s the long and short of it, really.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Less interested? Oh, goodness, no. No, I wouldn't say that at all. I mean, yes, this whole remodeling fiasco takes up a tremendous amount of my mental energy—how could it not, when you have people deliberately leaving nails exposed just waiting for you to snag a sweater on them?—but I certainly haven't become a hermit. I still do all the things I've always done. 

Just this past Tuesday, for instance, I went to my regular book club meeting over at Shirley's house. We were discussing that new historical fiction novel—the one set in Paris during the war, though honestly, I found the protagonist a bit grating, but that's neither here nor there. The point is, I was there, I brought my famous lemon bars, and I stayed for the whole two hours. And I still go to church every Sunday morning, bright and early for the 8:30 service, just like I have for the last forty years. I sit in the third pew on the left, right next to the Johnsons, and I always stay for coffee hour afterward, even though the coffee they serve is dreadfully weak. 

And my household routines haven't changed one bit, aside from having to clean up after that contractor, of course. I still do the laundry on Mondays, I still go to the grocery store on Thursday mornings to beat the weekend rush, and I still talk to my daughter on the phone every Sunday evening. We're actually planning a little family get-together for my grandson's birthday next month, and I've been looking at recipes for a dinosaur-shaped cake. So, no, I haven't lost interest in my life or my friends or my family. It's just that right now, I have this one very large, very stressful situation happening right in the middle of my home, and it demands a lot of my attention. But outside of dealing with that awful remodel and keeping an eye on those whispering neighbors, my life is exactly as it has always been. I'm still very much involved in my day-to-day activities, I assure you.

Oh, heavens, no. No, not at all. If anything, I am just completely exhausted by this whole ordeal. I'm lying there worrying about what the contractor is going to mess up next, or I'm replaying what I saw out the window, wondering exactly what those neighbors were whispering about by the mailboxes. I just lie there in the dark, looking up at the ceiling, feeling my stomach tie itself in knots over that crooked cabinet hinge they refuse to fix. I need my rest. By the time the 10 o'clock news is over, I am more than ready to turn in, maybe read a few pages of a book, and just close my eyes. So, no, I haven't felt unusually energetic in the slightest. This whole nightmare with the house has just completely drained whatever energy I do have, to be perfectly honest with you. I mean, I hear my husband snoring, like I said, and sometimes I hear the wind rattling that loose piece of siding the contractor promised to secure three months ago—which, of course, he hasn't done, and I'm sure he left it loose on purpose just so I'd have to listen to it every time there's a breeze. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Impossible? Oh, well... no, I wouldn't say impossible. I mean, what they're doing is certainly malicious, and highly coordinated, but it's not magic or science fiction or anything like that. It's just very, very sneaky. 

Like the contractor, for instance. There's nothing impossible about leaving a cabinet door slightly unhinged so it scrapes against the frame every single time I open it to get a tea bag. Anyone with a screwdriver and a mean streak could do that. And the neighbors, Mrs. Gable and Mr. Henderson—well, there's nothing impossible about standing by a mailbox and whispering. It's rude, certainly, and it's terribly upsetting to know they're coordinating their little stories about me, but it's just ordinary people doing awful things. 

I don't think they have special powers or hidden cameras in my walls or anything ridiculous like that. I mean, I did check behind the hallway mirror once, just to be absolutely certain, because the contractor spent an awfully long time hanging it, but there was nothing there. Just drywall and a rather sloppy patch job he tried to hide. So no, they aren't doing anything that defies the laws of physics. They're just using ordinary, everyday actions—like dropping off mail, or pretending to measure for tile, or walking that little yappy dog—to send me a message. To let me know they're watching, and that they're working together to drive me out of my own home. It's perfectly possible, doctor. It's just terribly, terribly cruel.

And my husband, he just sleeps right through it, snoring away, completely oblivious to it all. \n\nBut I'm certainly not bouncing off the walls or getting up to scrub the floors at midnight or anything like that. I need my rest. By the time the 10 o'clock news is over, I am more than ready to turn in, maybe read a few pages of a book, and just close my eyes. So, no, I haven't felt unusually energetic in the slightest. This whole nightmare with the house has just completely drained whatever energy I do have, to be perfectly honest with you. It takes a lot out of you, having to be constantly on your guard in your own home.\n\nDoctor: Have you heard voices when no one else was there?\n\nPatient: Voices? Oh, no, no, nothing like that. I mean, I hear my husband snoring, like I said, and sometimes I hear the wind rattling that loose piece of siding the contractor promised to secure three months ago—which, of course, he hasn't done, and I'm sure he left it loose on purpose just so I'd have to listen to it every time there's a breeze. But actual voices? People talking when there's no one in the room? No, absolutely not. \n\nI know what I hear, just like I know what I see. When I hear those neighbors talking by the mailboxes, they are really there. I can see Mrs. Gable in her awful pink tracksuit, and Mr. Henderson with his little dog, and I can hear the murmur of their voices. I can't always make out the exact words, mind you, because they drop their voices on purpose when I open the door, but they are real people having a real conversation about me. \n\nBut I certainly don't sit alone in my living room hearing phantom voices whispering in my ear. My hearing is perfectly fine, thank goodness. In fact, I had it tested just last year at Dr. Aris's office—my regular doctor, you know, over on Elm Street—and he said for a woman of sixty-seven, my hearing is quite sharp. Which is probably why I catch all the little sighs and muttered comments the contractor makes when he thinks I'm not paying attention. But no, doctor, I don't hear things that aren't there. I'm just very observant of the things that *are* there, even if my husband refuses to acknowledge them.\n\nDoctor: Aside from this situation with the contractor and your neighbors, have you had any other strong beliefs that other people said were not true?\n\nPatient: Oh, well... no, I wouldn't say so. Not at all. I mean, my husband and I have our little disagreements, certainly. Just last week we had a bit of a row over how to organize the pantry—which, frankly, is a mess right now because the contractor left the shelving half-finished, just bare wood with nails sticking out, if you can believe it. But that's just normal married life, isn't it? Disagreeing about where the soup cans go. \n\nBut as for big, strong beliefs that people say aren't true? No, nothing like that. I've always been a very practical, down-to-earth sort of person. I don't go in for conspiracy theories or wild ideas or anything of that nature. I just pay attention to what's going on around me. I pay my bills, I keep a tidy house—or at least I try to, despite the dust that contractor tracks in on purpose—and I mind my own business. \n\nIt’s really just this one specific situation. It’s so glaringly obvious, you see. Anyone with eyes in their head would see exactly what I see if they just spent a week in my house, watching how these people operate. But aside from this whole mess with the kitchen and the neighbors whispering by the mailboxes... no, there's absolutely nothing else. I’m just a normal woman trying to get her kitchen finished, and being thwarted at every turn. That’s the long and short of it, really.\n\nDoctor: Have you become much less interested in seeing other people or doing the things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Less interested? Oh, goodness, no. No, I wouldn't say that at all. I mean, yes, this whole remodeling fiasco takes up a tremendous amount of my mental energy—how could it not, when you have people deliberately leaving nails exposed just waiting for you to snag a sweater on them?—but I certainly haven't become a hermit. I still do all the things I've always done. \n\nJust this past Tuesday, for instance, I went to my regular book club meeting over at Shirley's house. We were discussing that new historical fiction novel—the one set in Paris during the war, though honestly, I found the protagonist a bit grating, but that's neither here nor there. The point is, I was there, I brought my famous lemon bars, and I stayed for the whole two hours. And I still go to church every Sunday morning, bright and early for the 8:30 service, just like I have for the last forty years. I sit in the third pew on the left, right next to the Johnsons, and I always stay for coffee hour afterward, even though the coffee they serve is dreadfully weak. \n\nAnd my household routines haven't changed one bit, aside from having to clean up after that contractor, of course. I still do the laundry on Mondays, I still go to the grocery store on Thursday mornings to beat the weekend rush, and I still talk to my daughter on the phone every Sunday evening. We're actually planning a little family get-together for my grandson's birthday next month, and I've been looking at recipes for a dinosaur-shaped cake. So, no, I haven't lost interest in my life or my friends or my family. It's just that right now, I have this one very large, very stressful situation happening right in the middle of my home, and it demands a lot of my attention. But outside of dealing with that awful remodel and keeping an eye on those whispering neighbors, my life is exactly as it has always been. I'm still very much involved in my day-to-day activities, I assure you.\n\nDoctor: Has anything happened in this situation that you believe would be impossible for ordinary people to do?\n\nPatient: Impossible? Oh, well... no, I wouldn't say impossible. I mean, what they're doing is certainly malicious, and highly coordinated, but it's not magic or science fiction or anything like that. It's just very, very sneaky. \n\nLike the contractor, for instance. There's nothing impossible about leaving a cabinet door slightly unhinged so it scrapes against the frame every single time I open it to get a tea bag. Anyone with a screwdriver and a mean streak could do that. And the neighbors, Mrs. Gable and Mr. Henderson—well, there's nothing impossible about standing by a mailbox and whispering. It's rude, certainly, and it's terribly upsetting to know they're coordinating their little stories about me, but it's just ordinary people doing awful things. \n\nI don't think they have special powers or hidden cameras in my walls or anything ridiculous like that. I mean, I did check behind the hallway mirror once, just to be absolutely certain, because the contractor spent an awfully long time hanging it, but there was nothing there. Just drywall and a rather sloppy patch job he tried to hide. So no, they aren't doing anything that defies the laws of physics. They're just using ordinary, everyday actions—like dropping off mail, or pretending to measure for tile, or walking that little yappy dog—to send me a message. To let me know they're watching, and that they're working together to drive me out of my own home. It's perfectly possible, doctor. It's just terribly, terribly cruel.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]

I'm still very much involved in my day-to-day activities, I assure you.\n\nDoctor: Has anything happened in this situation that you believe would be impossible for ordinary people to do?\n\nPatient: Impossible? Oh, well... no, I wouldn't say impossible. I mean, what they're doing is certainly malicious, and highly coordinated, but it's not magic or science fiction or anything like that. It's just very, very sneaky. \n\nLike the contractor, for instance. There's nothing impossible about leaving a cabinet door slightly unhinged so it scrapes against the frame every single time I open it to get a tea bag. Anyone with a screwdriver and a mean streak could do that. And the neighbors, Mrs. Gable and Mr. Henderson—well, there's nothing impossible about standing by a mailbox and whispering. It's rude, certainly, and it's terribly upsetting to know they're coordinating their little stories about me, but it's just ordinary people doing awful things. \n\nI don't think they have special powers or hidden cameras in my walls or anything ridiculous like that. I mean, I did check behind the hallway mirror once, just to be absolutely certain, because the contractor spent an awfully long time hanging it, but there was nothing there. Just drywall and a rather sloppy patch job he tried to hide. So no, they aren't doing anything that defies the laws of physics. They're just using ordinary, everyday actions—like dropping off mail, or pretending to measure for tile, or walking that little yappy dog—to send me a message. To let me know they're watching, and that they're working together to drive me out of my own home. It's perfectly possible, doctor. It's just terribly, terribly cruel.\n\n[Last Question]\nSince this began, have you had trouble managing your usual day-to-day responsibilities?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 67 / not_in_workforce / high_school\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Functional Impairment\nmanifestation: \"I’ve stopped trusting certain neighbors and workers, and my husband has had to take over more of the remodeling decisions because I keep questioning what people are really doing.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Trouble managing? Well, I wouldn't say I can't manage my usual chores—like I told you, I still do the laundry, I still cook our meals, although cooking is a nightmare with the kitchen in the state it's in. But... well, I suppose things have shifted a bit. Just in certain areas. 

It’s just that I’ve completely stopped trusting certain neighbors and the workers who come to the house. I mean, how could I? When you see the things I see, you can't just blindly hand over a cup of coffee to a man who you know is deliberately trying to ruin your baseboards. It used to be that I handled all the remodeling decisions. I was the one who picked out the backsplash—a lovely ceramic subway tile, very classic, not that trendy glass stuff that looks dated in five years—and I was the one who greeted the men when they arrived at 8:00 AM. 

But now? Now my husband has had to take over almost all of the remodeling decisions. I told him, I said, "Arthur, you are just going to have to be the one to speak to them from now on." Because if I do it, I just end up so frustrated. I keep questioning what these people are really doing. Every time the plumber asks where the main water shut-off valve is, I can't help but think, "Why do you want to know that? What exactly are you planning to turn off when we aren't looking?" 

So, Arthur does it now. He talks to the contractor, he handles the paperwork, and if Mrs. Gable or Mr. Henderson happen to wander over while the crew is here, Arthur goes out to the driveway to intercept them. It's just... it's too exhausting for me to constantly have to decipher everyone's true motives. I just can't be involved in the day-to-day of the kitchen project anymore, not when I know they're all coordinating behind my back. So yes, Arthur has had to step in and manage that whole side of things, just so I don't have to deal with the constant lying. But as for my own personal responsibilities, the house is still clean and dinner is still on the table at six o'clock sharp.

What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, it's nice to meet you too, doctor, though to be perfectly honest, my husband is the one who really pushed for this appointment. But I suppose I’m here because... well, it all goes back to our house. Specifically, the kitchen. We decided to do a remodel, just a simple update, you know, but for nearly two years now—almost twenty-four whole months of this absolute nightmare—I’ve been absolutely sure that the contractor we hired is doing something completely underhanded. \n\nAnd it’s not just him, which is the really upsetting part. It’s some of the people right on my block, too. I’ve seen them out the window, talking by the mailboxes, looking over at my house and then suddenly stopping when I open the front door. They’ve been quietly working together to cheat us, I just know it. They are deliberately trying to keep my kitchen half-wrong on purpose. The cabinet doors don't line up right, the grout is completely the wrong shade, the trim isn't finished... they leave it like that on purpose, just stringing the job along to mock me. \n\nAnd my husband, bless his heart, he just doesn't want to see the reality of it. He keeps pulling out this thick folder of receipts, sitting me down at the dining table and pointing at the line items and the dates, trying to show me that everything is accounted for. But no matter how many receipts my husband shows me, or how many ridiculous excuses the contractor gives him over the phone—blaming supply chains or weather or whatever new lie he comes up with—it doesn't change what's actually happening. I know what I see, and I know they're all in on it together.\n\nDoctor: During this time, have you had a period when you felt unusually energetic and needed much less sleep than usual?\n\nPatient: Energetic? Oh, heavens, no. No, not at all. If anything, I am just completely exhausted by this whole ordeal. I mean, I’m sixty-seven years old, I haven't worked in quite some time, and I certainly don't have the energy of a teenager anymore, that's for sure. \n\nI always try to get my full eight hours of sleep every single night. Now, I will admit, sometimes I do wake up in the middle of the night—usually right around 3:00 or 3:30 in the morning, which is just the worst time to wake up—but that's absolutely not because I have extra energy. It's just because my mind won't shut off. I'm lying there worrying about what the contractor is going to mess up next, or I'm replaying what I saw out the window, wondering exactly what those neighbors were whispering about by the mailboxes. I just lie there in the dark, looking up at the ceiling, feeling my stomach tie itself in knots over that crooked cabinet hinge they refuse to fix. And my husband, he just sleeps right through it, snoring away, completely oblivious to it all. \n\nBut I'm certainly not bouncing off the walls or getting up to scrub the floors at midnight or anything like that. I need my rest. By the time the 10 o'clock news is over, I am more than ready to turn in, maybe read a few pages of a book, and just close my eyes. So, no, I haven't felt unusually energetic in the slightest. This whole nightmare with the house has just completely drained whatever energy I do have, to be perfectly honest with you. It takes a lot out of you, having to be constantly on your guard in your own home.\n\nDoctor: Have you heard voices when no one else was there?\n\nPatient: Voices? Oh, no, no, nothing like that. I mean, I hear my husband snoring, like I said, and sometimes I hear the wind rattling that loose piece of siding the contractor promised to secure three months ago—which, of course, he hasn't done, and I'm sure he left it loose on purpose just so I'd have to listen to it every time there's a breeze. But actual voices? People talking when there's no one in the room? No, absolutely not. \n\nI know what I hear, just like I know what I see. When I hear those neighbors talking by the mailboxes, they are really there. I can see Mrs. Gable in her awful pink tracksuit, and Mr. Henderson with his little dog, and I can hear the murmur of their voices. I can't always make out the exact words, mind you, because they drop their voices on purpose when I open the door, but they are real people having a real conversation about me. \n\nBut I certainly don't sit alone in my living room hearing phantom voices whispering in my ear. My hearing is perfectly fine, thank goodness. In fact, I had it tested just last year at Dr. Aris's office—my regular doctor, you know, over on Elm Street—and he said for a woman of sixty-seven, my hearing is quite sharp. Which is probably why I catch all the little sighs and muttered comments the contractor makes when he thinks I'm not paying attention. But no, doctor, I don't hear things that aren't there. I'm just very observant of the things that *are* there, even if my husband refuses to acknowledge them.\n\nDoctor: Aside from this situation with the contractor and your neighbors, have you had any other strong beliefs that other people said were not true?\n\nPatient: Oh, well... no, I wouldn't say so. Not at all. I mean, my husband and I have our little disagreements, certainly. Just last week we had a bit of a row over how to organize the pantry—which, frankly, is a mess right now because the contractor left the shelving half-finished, just bare wood with nails sticking out, if you can believe it. But that's just normal married life, isn't it? Disagreeing about where the soup cans go. \n\nBut as for big, strong beliefs that people say aren't true? No, nothing like that. I've always been a very practical, down-to-earth sort of person. I don't go in for conspiracy theories or wild ideas or anything of that nature. I just pay attention to what's going on around me. I pay my bills, I keep a tidy house—or at least I try to, despite the dust that contractor tracks in on purpose—and I mind my own business. \n\nIt’s really just this one specific situation. It’s so glaringly obvious, you see. Anyone with eyes in their head would see exactly what I see if they just spent a week in my house, watching how these people operate. But aside from this whole mess with the kitchen and the neighbors whispering by the mailboxes... no, there's absolutely nothing else. I’m just a normal woman trying to get her kitchen finished, and being thwarted at every turn. That’s the long and short of it, really.\n\nDoctor: Have you become much less interested in seeing other people or doing the things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Less interested? Oh, goodness, no. No, I wouldn't say that at all. I mean, yes, this whole remodeling fiasco takes up a tremendous amount of my mental energy—how could it not, when you have people deliberately leaving nails exposed just waiting for you to snag a sweater on them?—but I certainly haven't become a hermit. I still do all the things I've always done. \n\nJust this past Tuesday, for instance, I went to my regular book club meeting over at Shirley's house. We were discussing that new historical fiction novel—the one set in Paris during the war, though honestly, I found the protagonist a bit grating, but that's neither here nor there. The point is, I was there, I brought my famous lemon bars, and I stayed for the whole two hours. We're actually planning a little family get-together for my grandson's birthday next month, and I've been looking at recipes for a dinosaur-shaped cake. So, no, I haven't lost interest in my life or my friends or my family. It's just that right now, I have this one very large, very stressful situation happening right in the middle of my home, and it demands a lot of my attention. Oh, heavens, no. No, nothing like that. My thoughts are my own, thank you very much. I mean, I know some people talk about things like that—you see it in those science fiction movies my grandson likes to watch, where people wear tin foil hats or whatever nonsense it is, but no. Although, I will say, sometimes it feels like they know what I'm thinking, but not because of telepathy or mind reading! No, it's just because they're so terribly observant of my habits. Like Mrs. Gable. She knows exactly what time I usually go out to get the mail—10:15, right after I finish my second cup of tea—so of course she’s out there, pretending to deadhead her petunias, just waiting for me. And the contractor, he knows I hate a mess, so he deliberately leaves his muddy boots right on the edge of the drop cloth where he knows I'll see them and get upset. 

So, they can anticipate my reactions, certainly. Anyone could if they watched someone closely enough, and they are definitely watching me. But my actual thoughts? No, those are safe inside my own head. I don't feel like anyone is broadcasting them or putting foreign ideas in there. I know exactly what I think, and I know exactly what they're doing. It’s just old-fashioned snooping and manipulation, doctor, not mind control. I assure you, my mind is perfectly sound and completely my own, even if they are trying to drive me out of it with all this stress!

Now my husband has had to take over almost all of the remodeling decisions. I told him, I said, \"Arthur, you are just going to have to be the one to speak to them from now on.\" Because if I do it, I just end up so frustrated. I keep questioning what these people are really doing. Every time the plumber asks where the main water shut-off valve is, I can't help but think, \"Why do you want to know that? What exactly are you planning to turn off when we aren't looking?\" \n\nSo, Arthur does it now. He talks to the contractor, he handles the paperwork, and if Mrs. Gable or Mr. Henderson happen to wander over while the crew is here, Arthur goes out to the driveway to intercept them. It's just... it's too exhausting for me to constantly have to decipher everyone's true motives. I just can't be involved in the day-to-day of the kitchen project anymore, not when I know they're all coordinating behind my back. So yes, Arthur has had to step in and manage that whole side of things, just so I don't have to deal with the constant lying. But as for my own personal responsibilities, the house is still clean and dinner is still on the table at six o'clock sharp.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel that your thoughts are being put into your mind, taken out, or made known to other people?\n\nPatient: Oh, heavens, no. No, nothing like that. My thoughts are my own, thank you very much. I mean, I know some people talk about things like that—you see it in those science fiction movies my grandson likes to watch, where people wear tin foil hats or whatever nonsense it is, but no. I don't feel like anyone is putting thoughts into my head or stealing them away. \n\nAlthough, I will say, sometimes it feels like they know what I'm thinking, but not because of telepathy or mind reading! No, it's just because they're so terribly observant of my habits. Like Mrs. Gable. She knows exactly what time I usually go out to get the mail—10:15, right after I finish my second cup of tea—so of course she’s out there, pretending to deadhead her petunias, just waiting for me. And the contractor, he knows I hate a mess, so he deliberately leaves his muddy boots right on the edge of the drop cloth where he knows I'll see them and get upset. \n\nSo, they can anticipate my reactions, certainly. Anyone could if they watched someone closely enough, and they are definitely watching me. But my actual thoughts? No, those are safe inside my own head. I don't feel like anyone is broadcasting them or putting foreign ideas in there. I know exactly what I think, and I know exactly what they're doing. It’s just old-fashioned snooping and manipulation, doctor, not mind control. I assure you, my mind is perfectly sound and completely my own, even if they are trying to drive me out of it with all this stress!\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

But actual voices? People talking when there's no one in the room? No, absolutely not. \n\nI know what I hear, just like I know what I see. I'm just very observant of the things that *are* there, even if my husband refuses to acknowledge them.\n\nDoctor: Aside from this situation with the contractor and your neighbors, have you had any other strong beliefs that other people said were not true?\n\nPatient: Oh, well... no, I wouldn't say so. Not at all. I mean, my husband and I have our little disagreements, certainly. I've always been a very practical, down-to-earth sort of person. I don't go in for conspiracy theories or wild ideas or anything of that nature. I just pay attention to what's going on around me. I pay my bills, I keep a tidy house—or at least I try to, despite the dust that contractor tracks in on purpose—and I mind my own business. \n\nIt’s really just this one specific situation. It’s so glaringly obvious, you see. Anyone with eyes in their head would see exactly what I see if they just spent a week in my house, watching how these people operate. But aside from this whole mess with the kitchen and the neighbors whispering by the mailboxes... no, there's absolutely nothing else. I’m just a normal woman trying to get her kitchen finished, and being thwarted at every turn. The point is, I was there, I brought my famous lemon bars, and I stayed for the whole two hours. And I still go to church every Sunday morning, bright and early for the 8:30 service, just like I have for the last forty years. I sit in the third pew on the left, right next to the Johnsons, and I always stay for coffee hour afterward, even though the coffee they serve is dreadfully weak. \n\nAnd my household routines haven't changed one bit, aside from having to clean up after that contractor, of course. I still do the laundry on Mondays, I still go to the grocery store on Thursday mornings to beat the weekend rush, and I still talk to my daughter on the phone every Sunday evening. We're actually planning a little family get-together for my grandson's birthday next month, and I've been looking at recipes for a dinosaur-shaped cake. So, no, I haven't lost interest in my life or my friends or my family. It's just that right now, I have this one very large, very stressful situation happening right in the middle of my home, and it demands a lot of my attention. But outside of dealing with that awful remodel and keeping an eye on those whispering neighbors, my life is exactly as it has always been. I'm still very much involved in my day-to-day activities, I assure you.\n\nDoctor: Has anything happened in this situation that you believe would be impossible for ordinary people to do?\n\nPatient: Impossible? I mean, I know some people talk about things like that—you see it in those science fiction movies my grandson likes to watch, where people wear tin foil hats or whatever nonsense it is, but no. I don't feel like anyone is putting thoughts into my head or stealing them away. \n\nAlthough, I will say, sometimes it feels like they know what I'm thinking, but not because of telepathy or mind reading! No, it's just because they're so terribly observant of my habits. Like Mrs. Gable. She knows exactly what time I usually go out to get the mail—10:15, right after I finish my second cup of tea—so of course she’s out there, pretending to deadhead her petunias, just waiting for me. And the contractor, he knows I hate a mess, so he deliberately leaves his muddy boots right on the edge of the drop cloth where he knows I'll see them and get upset. \n\nSo, they can anticipate my reactions, certainly. Anyone could if they watched someone closely enough, and they are definitely watching me. But my actual thoughts? No, those are safe inside my own head. I don't feel like anyone is broadcasting them or putting foreign ideas in there. I know exactly what I think, and I know exactly what they're doing. It’s just old-fashioned snooping and manipulation, doctor, not mind control. I assure you, my mind is perfectly sound and completely my own, even if they are trying to drive me out of it with all this stress!\n\n[Last Question]\nHave other people told you that your speech or behavior has become disorganized or hard to follow?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 67 / not_in_workforce / high_school\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Disorganized? Hard to follow? Well... no, no one has said anything quite like that. I mean, Arthur sometimes says I get a bit... well, he calls it "wound up." He'll say, "Martha, you're getting wound up again, take a breath," but that's just because I have so much to explain to him! When you're dealing with a situation as complicated as this one, with the neighbors and the contractors all doing these little, tiny things—you can't just explain it in one sentence. It takes time to lay it all out. 

Like yesterday, I was trying to tell him about the incident with the plumber's wrench. I had to explain where the wrench was left, which was right on my good floral hand towel, and then I had to explain how Mr. Henderson had just walked his dog past the front window not ten minutes prior, and how the two things were obviously connected because the plumber had been looking out that exact window. Arthur got a little impatient, I suppose. He said I was jumping around too much. But I wasn't disorganized, I was just trying to give him the complete picture! You can't understand the malice of leaving a greasy wrench on a floral towel unless you understand the timing of Mr. Henderson's dog walk, you see? 

So, no, I wouldn't say my speech is hard to follow, not if someone is actually listening and paying attention to the details. My daughter, Susan, she called on Sunday—she calls every Sunday at 4:00, very predictable—and I was telling her about the cabinet door, the one the contractor ruined, and she said, "Mom, you're obsessing, I can't follow all this about hinges and screws." But again, that's not me being disorganized! That's just Susan not wanting to hear about the reality of what's happening to her mother. She's always been a bit dismissive, ever since she was a teenager. She used to roll her eyes when I'd ask her to explain where she'd been, and she does the exact same thing now when I try to explain what Mrs. Gable is up to. 

So, people might say I talk about it a lot, or that I give too many details, but my thoughts are perfectly clear. I know exactly what I'm saying from beginning to end. It's just that other people don't always have the patience to listen to the whole story, doctor. They want everything in a neat little summary, but life—especially when people are conspiring against you—just doesn't fit into a neat little summary. It takes time to explain.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n9\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF22, F23.3, F20.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, it’s good to meet you. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, it's nice to meet you too, doctor, though to be perfectly honest, my husband is the one who really pushed for this appointment. But I suppose I’m here because... well, it all goes back to our house. Specifically, the kitchen. We decided to do a remodel, just a simple update, you know, but for nearly two years now—almost twenty-four whole months of this absolute nightmare—I’ve been absolutely sure that the contractor we hired is doing something completely underhanded. \n\nAnd it’s not just him, which is the really upsetting part. It’s some of the people right on my block, too. I’ve seen them out the window, talking by the mailboxes, looking over at my house and then suddenly stopping when I open the front door. They’ve been quietly working together to cheat us, I just know it. They are deliberately trying to keep my kitchen half-wrong on purpose. The cabinet doors don't line up right, the grout is completely the wrong shade, the trim isn't finished... they leave it like that on purpose, just stringing the job along to mock me. \n\nAnd my husband, bless his heart, he just doesn't want to see the reality of it. He keeps pulling out this thick folder of receipts, sitting me down at the dining table and pointing at the line items and the dates, trying to show me that everything is accounted for. But no matter how many receipts my husband shows me, or how many ridiculous excuses the contractor gives him over the phone—blaming supply chains or weather or whatever new lie he comes up with—it doesn't change what's actually happening. I know what I see, and I know they're all in on it together.\n\nDoctor: During this time, have you had a period when you felt unusually energetic and needed much less sleep than usual?\n\nPatient: Energetic? Oh, heavens, no. No, not at all. If anything, I am just completely exhausted by this whole ordeal. I mean, I’m sixty-seven years old, I haven't worked in quite some time, and I certainly don't have the energy of a teenager anymore, that's for sure. \n\nI always try to get my full eight hours of sleep every single night. Now, I will admit, sometimes I do wake up in the middle of the night—usually right around 3:00 or 3:30 in the morning, which is just the worst time to wake up—but that's absolutely not because I have extra energy. It's just because my mind won't shut off. I'm lying there worrying about what the contractor is going to mess up next, or I'm replaying what I saw out the window, wondering exactly what those neighbors were whispering about by the mailboxes. I just lie there in the dark, looking up at the ceiling, feeling my stomach tie itself in knots over that crooked cabinet hinge they refuse to fix. And my husband, he just sleeps right through it, snoring away, completely oblivious to it all. \n\nBut I'm certainly not bouncing off the walls or getting up to scrub the floors at midnight or anything like that. I need my rest. By the time the 10 o'clock news is over, I am more than ready to turn in, maybe read a few pages of a book, and just close my eyes. So, no, I haven't felt unusually energetic in the slightest. This whole nightmare with the house has just completely drained whatever energy I do have, to be perfectly honest with you. It takes a lot out of you, having to be constantly on your guard in your own home.\n\nDoctor: Have you heard voices when no one else was there?\n\nPatient: Voices? Oh, no, no, nothing like that. I mean, I hear my husband snoring, like I said, and sometimes I hear the wind rattling that loose piece of siding the contractor promised to secure three months ago—which, of course, he hasn't done, and I'm sure he left it loose on purpose just so I'd have to listen to it every time there's a breeze. But actual voices? People talking when there's no one in the room? No, absolutely not. \n\nI know what I hear, just like I know what I see. When I hear those neighbors talking by the mailboxes, they are really there. I can see Mrs. Gable in her awful pink tracksuit, and Mr. Henderson with his little dog, and I can hear the murmur of their voices. I can't always make out the exact words, mind you, because they drop their voices on purpose when I open the door, but they are real people having a real conversation about me. \n\nBut I certainly don't sit alone in my living room hearing phantom voices whispering in my ear. My hearing is perfectly fine, thank goodness. In fact, I had it tested just last year at Dr. Aris's office—my regular doctor, you know, over on Elm Street—and he said for a woman of sixty-seven, my hearing is quite sharp. Which is probably why I catch all the little sighs and muttered comments the contractor makes when he thinks I'm not paying attention. But no, doctor, I don't hear things that aren't there. I'm just very observant of the things that *are* there, even if my husband refuses to acknowledge them.\n\nDoctor: Aside from this situation with the contractor and your neighbors, have you had any other strong beliefs that other people said were not true?\n\nPatient: Oh, well... no, I wouldn't say so. Not at all. I mean, my husband and I have our little disagreements, certainly. Just last week we had a bit of a row over how to organize the pantry—which, frankly, is a mess right now because the contractor left the shelving half-finished, just bare wood with nails sticking out, if you can believe it. But that's just normal married life, isn't it? Disagreeing about where the soup cans go. \n\nBut as for big, strong beliefs that people say aren't true? No, nothing like that. I've always been a very practical, down-to-earth sort of person. I don't go in for conspiracy theories or wild ideas or anything of that nature. I just pay attention to what's going on around me. I pay my bills, I keep a tidy house—or at least I try to, despite the dust that contractor tracks in on purpose—and I mind my own business. \n\nIt’s really just this one specific situation. It’s so glaringly obvious, you see. Anyone with eyes in their head would see exactly what I see if they just spent a week in my house, watching how these people operate. But aside from this whole mess with the kitchen and the neighbors whispering by the mailboxes... no, there's absolutely nothing else. I’m just a normal woman trying to get her kitchen finished, and being thwarted at every turn. That’s the long and short of it, really.\n\nDoctor: Have you become much less interested in seeing other people or doing the things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Less interested? Oh, goodness, no. No, I wouldn't say that at all. I mean, yes, this whole remodeling fiasco takes up a tremendous amount of my mental energy—how could it not, when you have people deliberately leaving nails exposed just waiting for you to snag a sweater on them?—but I certainly haven't become a hermit. I still do all the things I've always done. \n\nJust this past Tuesday, for instance, I went to my regular book club meeting over at Shirley's house. We were discussing that new historical fiction novel—the one set in Paris during the war, though honestly, I found the protagonist a bit grating, but that's neither here nor there. The point is, I was there, I brought my famous lemon bars, and I stayed for the whole two hours. And I still go to church every Sunday morning, bright and early for the 8:30 service, just like I have for the last forty years. I sit in the third pew on the left, right next to the Johnsons, and I always stay for coffee hour afterward, even though the coffee they serve is dreadfully weak. \n\nAnd my household routines haven't changed one bit, aside from having to clean up after that contractor, of course. I still do the laundry on Mondays, I still go to the grocery store on Thursday mornings to beat the weekend rush, and I still talk to my daughter on the phone every Sunday evening. We're actually planning a little family get-together for my grandson's birthday next month, and I've been looking at recipes for a dinosaur-shaped cake. So, no, I haven't lost interest in my life or my friends or my family. It's just that right now, I have this one very large, very stressful situation happening right in the middle of my home, and it demands a lot of my attention. But outside of dealing with that awful remodel and keeping an eye on those whispering neighbors, my life is exactly as it has always been. I'm still very much involved in my day-to-day activities, I assure you.\n\nDoctor: Has anything happened in this situation that you believe would be impossible for ordinary people to do?\n\nPatient: Impossible? Oh, well... no, I wouldn't say impossible. I mean, what they're doing is certainly malicious, and highly coordinated, but it's not magic or science fiction or anything like that. It's just very, very sneaky. \n\nLike the contractor, for instance. There's nothing impossible about leaving a cabinet door slightly unhinged so it scrapes against the frame every single time I open it to get a tea bag. Anyone with a screwdriver and a mean streak could do that. And the neighbors, Mrs. Gable and Mr. Henderson—well, there's nothing impossible about standing by a mailbox and whispering. It's rude, certainly, and it's terribly upsetting to know they're coordinating their little stories about me, but it's just ordinary people doing awful things. \n\nI don't think they have special powers or hidden cameras in my walls or anything ridiculous like that. I mean, I did check behind the hallway mirror once, just to be absolutely certain, because the contractor spent an awfully long time hanging it, but there was nothing there. Just drywall and a rather sloppy patch job he tried to hide. So no, they aren't doing anything that defies the laws of physics. They're just using ordinary, everyday actions—like dropping off mail, or pretending to measure for tile, or walking that little yappy dog—to send me a message. To let me know they're watching, and that they're working together to drive me out of my own home. It's perfectly possible, doctor. It's just terribly, terribly cruel.\n\nDoctor: Since this began, have you had trouble managing your usual day-to-day responsibilities?\n\nPatient: Trouble managing? Well, I wouldn't say I can't manage my usual chores—like I told you, I still do the laundry, I still cook our meals, although cooking is a nightmare with the kitchen in the state it's in. But... well, I suppose things have shifted a bit. Just in certain areas. \n\nIt’s just that I’ve completely stopped trusting certain neighbors and the workers who come to the house. I mean, how could I? When you see the things I see, you can't just blindly hand over a cup of coffee to a man who you know is deliberately trying to ruin your baseboards. It used to be that I handled all the remodeling decisions. I was the one who picked out the backsplash—a lovely ceramic subway tile, very classic, not that trendy glass stuff that looks dated in five years—and I was the one who greeted the men when they arrived at 8:00 AM. \n\nBut now? Now my husband has had to take over almost all of the remodeling decisions. I told him, I said, \"Arthur, you are just going to have to be the one to speak to them from now on.\" Because if I do it, I just end up so frustrated. I keep questioning what these people are really doing. Every time the plumber asks where the main water shut-off valve is, I can't help but think, \"Why do you want to know that? What exactly are you planning to turn off when we aren't looking?\" \n\nSo, Arthur does it now. He talks to the contractor, he handles the paperwork, and if Mrs. Gable or Mr. Henderson happen to wander over while the crew is here, Arthur goes out to the driveway to intercept them. It's just... it's too exhausting for me to constantly have to decipher everyone's true motives. I just can't be involved in the day-to-day of the kitchen project anymore, not when I know they're all coordinating behind my back. So yes, Arthur has had to step in and manage that whole side of things, just so I don't have to deal with the constant lying. But as for my own personal responsibilities, the house is still clean and dinner is still on the table at six o'clock sharp.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel that your thoughts are being put into your mind, taken out, or made known to other people?\n\nPatient: Oh, heavens, no. No, nothing like that. My thoughts are my own, thank you very much. I mean, I know some people talk about things like that—you see it in those science fiction movies my grandson likes to watch, where people wear tin foil hats or whatever nonsense it is, but no. I don't feel like anyone is putting thoughts into my head or stealing them away. \n\nAlthough, I will say, sometimes it feels like they know what I'm thinking, but not because of telepathy or mind reading! No, it's just because they're so terribly observant of my habits. Like Mrs. Gable. She knows exactly what time I usually go out to get the mail—10:15, right after I finish my second cup of tea—so of course she’s out there, pretending to deadhead her petunias, just waiting for me. And the contractor, he knows I hate a mess, so he deliberately leaves his muddy boots right on the edge of the drop cloth where he knows I'll see them and get upset. \n\nSo, they can anticipate my reactions, certainly. Anyone could if they watched someone closely enough, and they are definitely watching me. But my actual thoughts? No, those are safe inside my own head. I don't feel like anyone is broadcasting them or putting foreign ideas in there. I know exactly what I think, and I know exactly what they're doing. It’s just old-fashioned snooping and manipulation, doctor, not mind control. I assure you, my mind is perfectly sound and completely my own, even if they are trying to drive me out of it with all this stress!\n\nDoctor: Have other people told you that your speech or behavior has become disorganized or hard to follow?\n\nPatient: Disorganized? Hard to follow? Well... no, no one has said anything quite like that. I mean, Arthur sometimes says I get a bit... well, he calls it \"wound up.\" He'll say, \"Martha, you're getting wound up again, take a breath,\" but that's just because I have so much to explain to him! When you're dealing with a situation as complicated as this one, with the neighbors and the contractors all doing these little, tiny things—you can't just explain it in one sentence. It takes time to lay it all out. \n\nLike yesterday, I was trying to tell him about the incident with the plumber's wrench. I had to explain where the wrench was left, which was right on my good floral hand towel, and then I had to explain how Mr. Henderson had just walked his dog past the front window not ten minutes prior, and how the two things were obviously connected because the plumber had been looking out that exact window. Arthur got a little impatient, I suppose. He said I was jumping around too much. But I wasn't disorganized, I was just trying to give him the complete picture! You can't understand the malice of leaving a greasy wrench on a floral towel unless you understand the timing of Mr. Henderson's dog walk, you see? \n\nSo, no, I wouldn't say my speech is hard to follow, not if someone is actually listening and paying attention to the details. I sit in the third pew on the left, right next to the Johnsons, and I always stay for coffee hour afterward, even though the coffee they serve is dreadfully weak. \n\nAnd my household routines haven't changed one bit, aside from having to clean up after that contractor, of course. I still do the laundry on Mondays, I still go to the grocery store on Thursday mornings to beat the weekend rush, and I still talk to my daughter on the phone every Sunday evening. We're actually planning a little family get-together for my grandson's birthday next month, and I've been looking at recipes for a dinosaur-shaped cake. So, no, I haven't lost interest in my life or my friends or my family. It's just that right now, I have this one very large, very stressful situation happening right in the middle of my home, and it demands a lot of my attention. But outside of dealing with that awful remodel and keeping an eye on those whispering neighbors, my life is exactly as it has always been. I'm still very much involved in my day-to-day activities, I assure you.\n\nDoctor: Has anything happened in this situation that you believe would be impossible for ordinary people to do?\n\nPatient: Impossible? Oh, well... no, I wouldn't say impossible. I mean, what they're doing is certainly malicious, and highly coordinated, but it's not magic or science fiction or anything like that. It's just very, very sneaky. \n\nLike the contractor, for instance. There's nothing impossible about leaving a cabinet door slightly unhinged so it scrapes against the frame every single time I open it to get a tea bag. Anyone with a screwdriver and a mean streak could do that. And the neighbors, Mrs. Gable and Mr. Henderson—well, there's nothing impossible about standing by a mailbox and whispering. It's rude, certainly, and it's terribly upsetting to know they're coordinating their little stories about me, but it's just ordinary people doing awful things. \n\nI don't think they have special powers or hidden cameras in my walls or anything ridiculous like that. I mean, I did check behind the hallway mirror once, just to be absolutely certain, because the contractor spent an awfully long time hanging it, but there was nothing there. Just drywall and a rather sloppy patch job he tried to hide. So no, they aren't doing anything that defies the laws of physics. They're just using ordinary, everyday actions—like dropping off mail, or pretending to measure for tile, or walking that little yappy dog—to send me a message. To let me know they're watching, and that they're working together to drive me out of my own home. It's perfectly possible, doctor. It's just terribly, terribly cruel.\n\nDoctor: Since this began, have you had trouble managing your usual day-to-day responsibilities?\n\nPatient: Trouble managing? Well, I wouldn't say I can't manage my usual chores—like I told you, I still do the laundry, I still cook our meals, although cooking is a nightmare with the kitchen in the state it's in. But... well, I suppose things have shifted a bit. Just in certain areas. \n\nIt’s just that I’ve completely stopped trusting certain neighbors and the workers who come to the house. I mean, how could I? When you see the things I see, you can't just blindly hand over a cup of coffee to a man who you know is deliberately trying to ruin your baseboards. It used to be that I handled all the remodeling decisions. I was the one who picked out the backsplash—a lovely ceramic subway tile, very classic, not that trendy glass stuff that looks dated in five years—and I was the one who greeted the men when they arrived at 8:00 AM. \n\nBut now? Now my husband has had to take over almost all of the remodeling decisions. I told him, I said, \"Arthur, you are just going to have to be the one to speak to them from now on.\" Because if I do it, I just end up so frustrated. I keep questioning what these people are really doing. Every time the plumber asks where the main water shut-off valve is, I can't help but think, \"Why do you want to know that? What exactly are you planning to turn off when we aren't looking?\" \n\nSo, Arthur does it now. He talks to the contractor, he handles the paperwork, and if Mrs. Gable or Mr. Henderson happen to wander over while the crew is here, Arthur goes out to the driveway to intercept them. It's just... it's too exhausting for me to constantly have to decipher everyone's true motives. I just can't be involved in the day-to-day of the kitchen project anymore, not when I know they're all coordinating behind my back. So yes, Arthur has had to step in and manage that whole side of things, just so I don't have to deal with the constant lying. But as for my own personal responsibilities, the house is still clean and dinner is still on the table at six o'clock sharp.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel that your thoughts are being put into your mind, taken out, or made known to other people?\n\nPatient: Oh, heavens, no. No, nothing like that. My thoughts are my own, thank you very much. I mean, I know some people talk about things like that—you see it in those science fiction movies my grandson likes to watch, where people wear tin foil hats or whatever nonsense it is, but no. I don't feel like anyone is putting thoughts into my head or stealing them away. \n\nAlthough, I will say, sometimes it feels like they know what I'm thinking, but not because of telepathy or mind reading! No, it's just because they're so terribly observant of my habits. Like Mrs. Gable. She knows exactly what time I usually go out to get the mail—10:15, right after I finish my second cup of tea—so of course she’s out there, pretending to deadhead her petunias, just waiting for me. And the contractor, he knows I hate a mess, so he deliberately leaves his muddy boots right on the edge of the drop cloth where he knows I'll see them and get upset. \n\nSo, they can anticipate my reactions, certainly. Anyone could if they watched someone closely enough, and they are definitely watching me. But my actual thoughts? No, those are safe inside my own head. I don't feel like anyone is broadcasting them or putting foreign ideas in there. I know exactly what I think, and I know exactly what they're doing. It’s just old-fashioned snooping and manipulation, doctor, not mind control. I assure you, my mind is perfectly sound and completely my own, even if they are trying to drive me out of it with all this stress!\n\nDoctor: Have other people told you that your speech or behavior has become disorganized or hard to follow?\n\nPatient: Disorganized? Hard to follow? Well... no, no one has said anything quite like that. I mean, Arthur sometimes says I get a bit... well, he calls it \"wound up.\" He'll say, \"Martha, you're getting wound up again, take a breath,\" but that's just because I have so much to explain to him! When you're dealing with a situation as complicated as this one, with the neighbors and the contractors all doing these little, tiny things—you can't just explain it in one sentence. It takes time to lay it all out. \n\nLike yesterday, I was trying to tell him about the incident with the plumber's wrench. I had to explain where the wrench was left, which was right on my good floral hand towel, and then I had to explain how Mr. Henderson had just walked his dog past the front window not ten minutes prior, and how the two things were obviously connected because the plumber had been looking out that exact window. Arthur got a little impatient, I suppose. He said I was jumping around too much. But I wasn't disorganized, I was just trying to give him the complete picture! You can't understand the malice of leaving a greasy wrench on a floral towel unless you understand the timing of Mr. Henderson's dog walk, you see? \n\nSo, no, I wouldn't say my speech is hard to follow, not if someone is actually listening and paying attention to the details. My daughter, Susan, she called on Sunday—she calls every Sunday at 4:00, very predictable—and I was telling her about the cabinet door, the one the contractor ruined, and she said, \"Mom, you're obsessing, I can't follow all this about hinges and screws.\" But again, that's not me being disorganized! That's just Susan not wanting to hear about the reality of what's happening to her mother. She's always been a bit dismissive, ever since she was a teenager. She used to roll her eyes when I'd ask her to explain where she'd been, and she does the exact same thing now when I try to explain what Mrs. Gable is up to. \n\nSo, people might say I talk about it a lot, or that I give too many details, but my thoughts are perfectly clear. I know exactly what I'm saying from beginning to end. It's just that other people don't always have the patience to listen to the whole story, doctor. They want everything in a neat little summary, but life—especially when people are conspiring against you—just doesn't fit into a neat little summary. It takes time to explain.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

Three months? Oh, heavens, I wish it had only been three months. If it were only three months, I might still have some of my patience left! No, no, it’s been... well, let’s see. It has been much, much longer than that. 

It all started—the real disruption, I mean—when we finally began the kitchen and garden remodeling. Arthur and I had talked about updating the house for years, and we finally took the plunge. That was... goodness, it’s been about a year and nine months now. Yes, exactly a year and nine months. I know the timeline perfectly because I keep all the original contracts and invoices in a blue binder in the den. 

The very first day they came to tear up the old garden beds and start knocking out the drywall in the kitchen, that's when the nightmare began. You open up your home, you know? You expect a mess, you expect some dust and noise, and you expect your routine to be disrupted. But you don't expect the people you've hired to start actively working against you. That’s when I first started noticing the looks they would give each other, and the way Mrs. Gable suddenly took such a keen interest in her side yard the exact same week we broke ground. 

So, to answer your question, yes, it has been continuously going on for a year and nine months. Every single day since that remodeling started disrupting my house. It never stops, not for a single week. Even on the weekends or holidays when the crew isn't supposed to be there, I can see the neighbors out there inspecting the progress, taking their little mental notes to report back. It’s just been a year and nine months of absolute exhaustion, doctor, just a continuous, never-ending campaign against me. You'd think a kitchen project would be finished by now, wouldn't you? But of course, they keep finding reasons to drag it out.
